=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a string S of length N and a string T of length M, both consisting of digits from 1 to 9.
You will perform the following operation for k=1,2,\ldots,M in order:

- Choose an integer i such that 1 \le i \le N. Then, replace the i-th character of S with the k-th character of T.

Find the maximum possible value of the resulting string S interpreted as an integer after performing the M operations.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
S
T

Output

Print the maximum possible value of the resulting string S interpreted as an integer after performing the M operations.

Constraints


- 1 \le N,M \le 10^6
- N and M are integers.
- S is a string of length N consisting of digits from 1 through 9.
- T is a string of length M consisting of digits from 1 through 9.

Sample Input 1

3 3
191
325

Sample Output 1

593

The following sequence of operations is optimal:

- For k=1: Choose i=3. Then, S = 193.
- For k=2: Choose i=1. Then, S = 293.
- For k=3: Choose i=1. Then, S = 593.

In this case, the value of S interpreted as an integer is 593, which is the maximum.

Sample Input 2

3 9
191
998244353

Sample Output 2

993

Sample Input 3

11 13
31415926535
2718281828459

Sample Output 3

98888976555

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Thus algorithm:

6. For each char ch in S:
   s = int(ch)
   best = -1
   # find largest d from 9 down to s+1 such that (opt[d]>0) or (not forced_used and d==a)
   We can loop d from 9 down to s+1 (max 9 iterations) per position => O(9N) = 9e6, fine for 1e6. Need also know if forced candidate available. But if d==a and not forced_used, available. If opt[d]>0 available. Note if d==a and opt[a]>0 and forced not used, available; we should use forced not optional. If forced_used, opt[a] maybe >0.
   If best != -1:
      final_chars.append(str(best))
      if best == a and not forced_used:
          forced_used = True
      else:
          opt[best] -= 1
   else:
      final_chars.append(ch)

If there is a later position with S<a but no optional >a, greedy would use forced there before end, so forced used. If later position with S<a and optional >a, greedy uses optional there, final >a; forced may remain. Then at end if there is earlier S=a, place forced there no change, final includes optional at later. Is that feasible? Example S=[5,1], optional token9, forced5. Greedy pos1 S=5 no d>5 (forced=5 not >), keep5. pos2 S=1, optional9>1 use9 -> [5,9]. forced unused. End place forced at pos1 (final=5) no change -> [5,9]. Is this optimal? Operations: mandatory 5 at pos1, optional9 at pos2, final 59. Alternative use forced at pos2=5, optional9 at pos1? pos1 S=5, 9>5 -> [9,5], which is larger! Wait! Let's analyze carefully.

S=[5,1], T such that optional token 9, forced a=5. We can choose final assignment: positions:
- Option A: pos1=5 (forced), pos2=9 (optional) => 59.
- Option B: pos1=9 (optional), pos2=5 (forced) => 95.
Option B is better! Our greedy failed? Let's simulate greedy rule "choose largest d > S[i] among available including forced":
pos1 S=5. Available optional 9 >5, forced 5 not >5. Largest d>5 is 9. Greedy would use optional 9 at pos1, not keep. I mistakenly said no d>5? There is optional9. So greedy uses 9 at pos1 -> [9,1]. pos2 S=1, forced5>1 use forced -> [9,5]. Final 95. Good. So okay.

What if optional token 4, forced5, S=[5,1]. pos1 no d>5 (forced5 not >), keep5. pos2 S=1, forced5>1 use forced -> [5,5]. final55. Alternative optional4 at pos2, forced5 at pos1 =>55? Actually pos1=5, pos2=4 =>54, worse. Good.

What if S=[5,1], optional token6, forced5. pos1 use6 -> [6,1], pos2 forced5 ->65. Alternative forced pos1=5, optional pos2=6 =>56. 65 better. Good.

So greedy seems robust.

Need ensure counts of optional tokens after using forced: If candidate best == a and forced not used, we mark forced_used and do NOT decrement opt[a]. But what if there are optional tokens of digit a and we use forced at a position where S < a; later positions with S < a could use optional a? Yes opt[a] remains cnt[a]-1. Good. If candidate best > a, decrement opt[best]. If candidate best == a but forced already used, decrement opt[a]. If candidate best == a and forced not used but opt[a] also >0, using forced is correct; no decrement optional. Good.

But there is subtlety: At position i, if best == a and forced not used, we use forced. But what if there is an optional token of digit a and no other, and using forced now consumes the "mandatory occurrence"; later if we need another a token, optional remains. Fine.

Now, is the "available" condition for forced token simply not forced_used and a > S[i]? Yes, because using forced at position i would set digit a; if a > S[i], it improves current digit. If a <= S[i], using forced now would not improve; greedy would never choose it as best > S[i]. But could using forced now with a <= S[i] be necessary to allow an optional token > S[i] to be used later? No, using forced now would make current digit a (possibly lower than S[i]), which is worse than keeping S[i] or using optional. Since forced can be used later, no need. If no later and forced remains, end placement handles.

Now, after loop if forced unused, we need place forced at a position. But our greedy final_chars may have some positions with optional replacements and some original. We need choose position to set to a. We argued choose any position with final digit a if exists; else rightmost. But is it possible that the rightmost position has final digit < a? Wait if forced unused, can any final digit be < a? Let's prove. At each position:
- If S[i] < a:
   - If forced unused and no optional >a? Then a is available and >S[i], so best at least a, greedy would use forced (or optional >a). Thus final digit >= a.
   - If forced already used, optional tokens maybe; final digit could be <a? Suppose forced used earlier, at a later position S=1, a=5, optional tokens none. Greedy no d>1, final remains1 < a. So after forced used, final digits can be <a. But in the branch forced unused at end, forced was not used earlier, so for every position with S[i]<a, greedy must have used an optional token >a (since if no optional >a, it would have used forced). Thus final digit >a. If S[i]>=a, final digit >=a. So yes all final digits >=a. Good.
- If S[i]=a, final a.
Thus if forced unused, no final digit <a. So placing forced at rightmost if no final a causes decrease from >a to a, but no alternative with less lexicographic damage than rightmost? What if there is a position with final digit = a? Then no decrease. If none, all final >a. Rightmost best.

But wait: What if there is a position with final digit = a, but it's not the rightmost; placing forced there no change. But if we place forced at rightmost, final would decrease. So choose final a position. However, is it valid to "use" forced at a position whose final digit a came from original S, but that position may have been selected by an optional token earlier? No, if final digit a and forced unused, it must be original S=a and not selected by optional. Because optional a not used before forced. So okay.

But what if final digit a appears at a position that was originally S=a but we also used an optional token of digit >a at that position? Then final digit would >a, not a. So no.

Now, is there a scenario where forced unused and there is a position with final digit a, but choosing it for forced would make the final string not correspond to any valid sequence because the time M operation at that position would overwrite an earlier optional token assigned to same position? If final digit a is original S=a and no optional token at that position, then operation M can choose it; earlier operations not assigned there. Feasible. If final digit a appears because we set it to a? We didn't. Good.

Need consider if using all optional tokens greedily can consume a token that would be better saved to avoid forced damage? At end forced damage at rightmost; if rightmost was improved by a high optional token, forced overwrites it. Could it be better to not use that optional token at rightmost, leaving rightmost original lower, and use the optional token at an earlier position? Greedy processes left to right; if optional token could improve an earlier position, it would have been used there before reaching rightmost. If rightmost is the first position where token is beneficial, then using it there improves final until forced overwrites it. Option change pos N-1: ... a, Y.

Now, is the reduction to multiset with mandatory t_M fully correct? Need revisit feasibility of using any optional token from T indices while mandatory M selected. Suppose optional token digit d corresponds to an index k < M. If we assign optional token to position p and mandatory to position q, we need schedule operations such that final last times are k for p and M for q. For times not selected, can assign to q (last M) maybe. If k is before M, and we assign q at all other times including after k, q's last is M. At time k, choose p. At time M, choose q. Feasible. If optional token index k >? Only M is last; all other indices < M. So yes. What if optional token digit d from an index k that is after some other selected optional index l; no issue. Now, one subtlety: We assumed we can use at most one token per position and each T index at most once. But operation sequence allows a position to be overwritten multiple times; final token is last selected time. We model final assignment as selected times K. The number of selected times |K| equals number of positions that are touched at least once and not overwritten? Actually every touched position has a last selected time in K. So |K| = number of touched positions. Since M operations can touch at most M positions, but N positions. We can choose up to min(N,M) selected times? Wait K size cannot exceed M obviously, and cannot exceed N. Our token pool size is M; we can use at most N tokens. But if M < N, we have only M operations, so at most M positions can be touched? Actually each operation touches one position; a position can be touched multiple times, so number of touched positions ≤ M. Thus final T-derived positions ≤ M. Our pool size M, and r≤N; but also r≤M because selected times subset of T length M. Since we only have cnt total M tokens, using more than M impossible. We can use up to M tokens. Our greedy may use at most M optional+forced tokens because counts sum M. Good. But if N > M, many positions remain original. Fine.

Need consider if we can choose r=0? No, mandatory M means at least one selected time. But if N? N≥1. So r≥1. Our algorithm always uses forced eventually (either in loop or end). Good.

Now, is there any possibility to use T[M] mandatory but not as final digit because operation M overwrites a position that was already assigned an earlier selected time, making that earlier selected time not final? Wait operation M always final for the position chosen at M. So T[M] must be final at some position. Thus mandatory. Good.

Now, let's think if the final string can be characterized as "choose up to N positions to replace with digits from T, with one mandatory T[M]" exactly. We should maybe mention in final? But final answer only code. Need ensure code correct.

Let's attempt to prove greedy more formally for ourselves:
At any step i, suppose we have not processed positions i..N. The forced token may be unused or used. Optional tokens counts. We want lexicographically maximize suffix given prefix fixed. The digit at position i can be:
- S[i] if no token assigned.
- d if an available token d assigned.
We can always postpone forced token to a later position if not used; so any optional token d>S[i] can be used at i without making future infeasible (forced can be placed later). Thus the maximum possible digit at i is max(S[i], largest available token d with d>S[i]). If that digit is S[i] (no beneficial token), we should leave S[i] because any token ≤S[i] would make current digit ≤S[i], and lexicographically not better; future can still use forced. If there is beneficial token, choosing the largest such token maximizes current digit; any solution with smaller current digit is worse regardless of future. If multiple tokens same digit, choose forced if it is that digit to satisfy mandatory; otherwise optional. This greedy maintains feasibility. At the end, if forced unused, we must assign it to some position. Since all previous decisions were optimal for the case where forced is not used yet? Need ensure end placement doesn't invalidate previous greedy optimality. We can view end placement as choosing a position to decrease to a. We argued choose position with final digit a if exists, else rightmost. But is it possible that an earlier decision to use an optional token at a position that later gets overwritten by forced was suboptimal because we could have used forced there and saved optional token for another position? We considered but let's prove with exchange.

Suppose after greedy, forced unused. Let F be final before forced. All F[i] ≥ a. If there exists j with F[j]=a, placing forced there yields final F unchanged, which is optimal because no decrease. If no F[j]=a, all F[i]>a. Greedy final before forced is lexicographically maximum among strings achievable using optional tokens only (without forced)? Actually greedy with optional tokens only (no forced) would produce F? Let's see: Since forced unused, at every position where S[i]<a, greedy used an optional token >a; at positions S[i]≥a, it used optional tokens >S[i] if available. This is exactly the same as greedy with optional tokens only, because forced was never chosen (a never > S[i] when no optional? Wait if S[i]<a and no optional >a, forced would be chosen; since forced unused, every S[i]<a had optional >a. So forced was never the best candidate; the algorithm behaved as optional-only). Thus F is the lexicographically maximum string achievable using only optional tokens (with counts opt). Now we must place forced a somewhere. If no F[j]=a, all F[j]>a. We need choose one position to set to a. To maximize lexicographically, choose the rightmost position, because for any two positions i<j, setting i yields at position i a < F[i], while setting j leaves F[i] unchanged; since F[i]>a, setting j is better. Thus rightmost. If F[j]=a, setting that position leaves F unchanged, which is best. This proof relies on F being optional-only greedy. Is F indeed optional-only greedy? Need check if forced unused but at some position S[i]<a and optional token >a was available; greedy chose optional >a. If optional token >a not available, forced would be used. So yes.

But wait, what if at some position S[i]<a, there is optional token of digit a (opt[a]>0) and forced unused. Greedy candidate largest d>S[i] includes a (both forced and optional). It would choose forced a (since best maybe a if no >a). Then forced would be used, not unused. So if forced unused, no optional a used and no forced; if S[i]<a and no optional >a, forced would be used. Thus S[i]<a positions must have optional >a. Good.

Now, one more subtlety: The optional-only greedy F may use a token that is also needed to make F[j]=a? No, F[j]=a only original S=a.

Let's test a case where forced unused and no final a, choose rightmost. Example S=[9,8], T mandatory a=1, optional token? Suppose T has a=1 and optional 9? S=[9,8], opt9. Greedy optional-only: pos1 S=9 no d>9, pos2 S=8 opt9>8 use9 -> F=[9,9]. forced unused, no F=1, choose rightmost pos2 set1 -> [9,1]. Is optimal? Tokens: mandatory1, optional9. Use optional9 at pos2? final pos2=9, forced1 at pos1 -> [1,9] worse; forced1 at pos2 -> [9,1]. Could use optional9 at pos1? S1=9 no improvement but if use optional9 at pos1, forced1 at pos2 -> [9,1] same? Actually optional9 at pos1 no change, but consumes token; final [9,1]. Same. Could use forced1 at pos1, optional9 at pos2? [1,9] worse. So [9,1] best. Good.

What if S=[8,9], opt9, a=1. Greedy optional-only: pos1 S=8 opt9>8 use9 -> [9,9]; pos2 S=9 no. forced unused, no1, choose rightmost pos2 set1 -> [9,1]. Alternative forced1 pos1, optional9 pos2? S2=9 no improve -> [1,9]; worse. Alternative optional9 pos1, forced1 pos2 -> [9,1]. Good.

What if S=[8,8], opt9, a=1. Greedy optional-only: pos1 use9 -> [9,8]; pos2 S=8 no opt (used) -> [9,8]. forced unused, no1, choose rightmost pos2 set1 -> [9,1]. Alternative forced1 pos1, optional9 pos2 -> [1,9] worse. Alternative optional9 pos2, forced1 pos1 -> [1,9]? pos1=1,pos2=9 =>19, worse. [9,1] best. Good.

Now, what if optional-only greedy F has F[i]=a at some position, but that position is not rightmost; choose it no change. Example S=[1,9], a=1, opt9. Greedy optional-only: pos1 S=1 opt9>1 use9 -> [9,9]; pos2 S=9 no. F no1. Choose rightmost pos2 set1 -> [9,1]. Alternative forced1 at pos1 no change? Wait if forced1 at pos1, optional9 at pos2? Use 9 pos1, mandatory1 pos2 original1 =>91. Could use mandatory1 pos1, optional9 pos2 =>19, worse. Good.

Now, let's consider if optional-only greedy F might use an optional token at a position that originally was a, making F[i]>a, while another position originally a remains. If forced unused, choose an original a position no change. Example S=[1,1], a=1, opt9. F=[9,1], choose pos2. Good.

Now, what if there is no position with F[i]=a and all F[i]>a, but rightmost position is not the one with smallest decrease? Lexicographic says rightmost. Suppose F=[9,8], a=5. Change pos1 -> [5,8], change pos2 -> [9,5]. Compare [9,5] > [5,8] because pos1 9>5. So rightmost. Good.

Now, is there any case where after greedy forced used, we might have used forced at a position but later optional tokens could have produced a larger digit at that position, and forced could be placed later at a position with F=a? Greedy chooses largest d>S[i]; if forced used at i, it means forced a was the largest beneficial token available. If there was an optional token >a available, greedy would have used that instead. So no. If forced a used at i, any later position with S<a and no optional >a? forced already used, so may remain low; but lexicographic current position improved to a, which is better than any later improvement. Good.

Let's think about the "available token" counts and forced token if a appears multiple times. Suppose forced a unused, and at position i S[i]<a, available optional tokens: none >a, but opt[a] >0. Greedy best = a (forced). It uses forced, not optional. Later positions may need optional a; opt[a] remains. Good. If instead we used optional a and saved forced for later, final same digit at i, forced later maybe at a position with S=a no change; could yield same or better? Example S=[1,1], a=5, opt5 (another 5 in T), no other. Greedy uses forced at pos1 -> final5, pos2? forced used, opt5 available but 5 not >1? Wait S2=1, 5>1, so at pos2 candidate opt5>1, use optional5 -> final55. If we used optional5 at pos1 and forced5 at pos2, final55 same. So using forced vs optional same digit doesn't matter for final if both available. Our greedy uses forced first; okay. If there is only one occurrence of a (mandatory), then no optional a; after forced used, later S<a cannot be improved to a. But if we had used optional? none. So okay. If there are multiple a, using forced first may leave optional a for later, which is good. If we used optional first and forced later, final same multiset of a's. No difference. So preference forced is fine.

Now, let's examine the problem constraints: N,M up to 1e6, digits 1-9. Python O(N*9) okay. Need memory: S string length 1e6, T length 1e6. Reading input via sys.stdin.buffer.read().split() gives list of bytes, okay memory maybe ~2MB + overhead? Actually split creates list of 3 items, S and T as bytes; fine. We can process S bytes. Output list of chars length N maybe 1e6, okay. We can also build bytearray for efficiency. Need final code.

Let's decide implementation details.

Pseudo:

import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    N = int(data[0]); M = int(data[1])
    S = data[2].decode()? We can keep bytes.
    T = data[3]
    cnt = [0]*10
    for b in T:
        cnt[b - 48] += 1
    a = T[-1] - 48
    opt = cnt[:]  # optional counts
    opt[a] -= 1
    forced_used = False
    res = bytearray(N)  # or list of ints
    # We need final_chars to check for a at end. We can store digits as ints in list? bytearray of ascii.
    # For each position, compute best.
    # Since digits 1-9, S bytes are ascii.
    for idx, b in enumerate(S):
        s = b - 48
        best = -1
        # loop d from 9 down to s+1
        # Need check opt[d] > 0 or (not forced_used and d == a)
        # But if d == a and not forced_used, available. If opt[d] >0 also available.
        # We want largest d. Since loop descending, first available is best.
        for d in range(9, s, -1):  # 9 down to s+1
            if opt[d] > 0 or (not forced_used and d == a):
                best = d
                break
        if best != -1:
            res[idx] = 48 + best
            if best == a and not forced_used:
                forced_used = True
            else:
                opt[best] -= 1
        else:
            res[idx] = b
    if not forced_used:
        # Find a position with res digit == a
        # Need set forced. If any position res[idx] == 48+a, set? Actually no change, but we must mark? final same.
        # We can search from right? If there is any, choose rightmost such position? If multiple, any no change. But if choose a position with res==a, final unchanged. Need ensure if res==a at some position, setting to a no change. We don't actually need modify res. But we must "use" forced; final string same. So if any res==a, do nothing.
        # Else choose rightmost position and set to a.
        # But wait: If there is a position with res==a, do nothing. If not, set res[-1] = 48+a.
        # However, is it possible that res[-1] already a? That would be found. So if no found, res[-1] != a.
        # We can search from right for res[idx] == 48+a. If found, no change. Else res[-1] = 48+a.
        # But is choosing rightmost position always res[-1]? Yes if no res==a, all >a, rightmost overall. If there is res==a, no change. So we can simply:
        # if 48+a not in res: res[-1] = 48+a
        # But checking membership in bytearray O(N), okay. However, if res contains a, no change. If not, set last.
        # Is it possible res contains a but not as original? We argued no optional a before forced, but if forced_used false, no optional a used. But what if res contains a because S[i]=a and we didn't replace; yes. So membership check okay.
        if (48 + a) not in res:
            res[-1] = 48 + a
        # If membership true, no change. But wait, what if res contains a at some position, but that position is not the rightmost; no change final. Good.
    sys.stdout.buffer.write(res)

But let's verify with code: In loop, if best == a and not forced_used, we set forced_used True and do NOT decrement opt. So we never use optional a before forced. If best > a, opt[best] decremented. If best < a impossible because loop d>s and if s<a, best could be a or >a; if s>=a, best>s>=a so best>a. Thus no optional token with digit a used. So res==a only original S. Good. If forced_used false, no optional a used. So placing forced at that position means operation M chooses that position; but was that position maybe selected by an optional token of different digit earlier and then final original a? No, if selected by optional token, final would be that token digit, not a. So not selected. Good.

N=2, M=2. T = t1 t2, a=t2. Operations: k1 choose pos, k2 choose pos. Final: if choose same pos at k1 and k2, final one pos=t2, other original. If choose different, final pos1=t2 or t1 depending choices. Actually with M=2, possible final strings:
- k1 pos1, k2 pos1: [t2, S2]
- k1 pos2, k2 pos2: [S1, t2]
- k1 pos1, k2 pos2: [t1, t2]
- k1 pos2, k2 pos1: [t2, t1]
So final can have:
- one position t2, other original (if both ops same pos)
- pos1=t1, pos2=t2
- pos1=t2, pos2=t1
Our model: choose subset K containing {2}. If |K|=1, K={2}, assign t2 to one position, other original. If |K|=2, K={1,2}, assign t1 and t2 to two positions in any order. This matches. Greedy should find max among these.

But we proved no final digit <a if forced unused. Let's double-check with a case where forced_used False and S[i] < a but optional token >a not available, but there is optional token equal to a? If S[i]<a and opt[a]>0, then forced a is candidate and >S[i], so forced would be used. So forced_used True. If opt[a]=0 and no optional >a, forced used. Thus no S[i]<a remains. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where forced_used False and S[i] < a but optional token >a used, final >a. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where forced_used False and S[i] = a but optional token >a used, final >a, so no a at that position. If another S=a remains, a in res. If all S=a positions improved, a not in res. Then all res >a. Rightmost. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where forced_used False and S[i] > a but optional token >S[i] used, final >a. Good.

Now, let's consider if the algorithm's greedy optional-only F when forced unused is lexicographically maximum among strings using optional tokens only, but we also have the constraint that if forced unused, the final string after placing forced at rightmost might not be lexicographically maximum among all strings with forced because maybe an earlier decision to use optional token at a position that will be overwritten by forced could be changed to use a different optional token at an earlier position? We argued no. Let's try to find a counterexample with a swap.

Suppose N=3, S=[1,1,1], optional tokens [9,8], forced a=7. Greedy optional-only: pos1 use9, pos2 use8, pos3 keep1. forced unused, a not in res, set pos3=7 ->987. Is this optimal? Tokens mandatory7, optional9,8. Use 9 pos1,8 pos2,7 pos3 =>987. Could use 9 pos1,7 pos2,8 pos3 =>978, worse. Could use 8 pos1,9 pos2,7 pos3 =>897, worse. 987 best. Good.

Suppose S=[1,1,1], optional tokens [9], forced a=7. Greedy: pos1 use9, pos2 forced7 (since no optional >7? Actually forced7>1, best7) ->97, pos3 keep1 =>971. Is optimal? Tokens mandatory7, optional9. Use9 pos1,7 pos2 =>971. Alternative 7 pos1,9 pos2 =>791, worse. 971 best. Good.

Suppose S=[1,1,1], optional tokens [8], forced a=7. Greedy: pos1 use8, pos2 forced7 ->871. Alternative 7 pos1,8 pos2 =>781, worse. Good.

Suppose S=[1,1,1], optional tokens [6], forced a=7. Greedy: pos1 forced7 (since 7>6) ->7, pos2 use6? Wait after forced used, opt6 available, pos2 S=1, 6>1 use6 ->76, pos3 keep1 =>761. Alternative use6 pos1, forced7 pos2 =>671, worse. 761 best. Good.

Suppose S=[1,1,1], optional tokens [6,5], forced a=7. Greedy: pos1 forced7, pos2 use6, pos3 use5? 5>1 yes ->765. Alternative use6 pos1,7 pos2,5 pos3 =>675, worse. 765 best. Good.

Now, consider if optional token >a at early position and forced at end rightmost. Example S=[1,1,1], optional [9], forced a=7. Greedy: pos1 use9, pos2 forced7, pos3 keep1 =>971. If instead pos1 use9, pos2 keep1, pos3 forced7 =>917, worse. So forced used at first beneficial position after optional. Good.

Now, consider if optional token >a at early and no later S<a, forced unused at end. Example S=[1,9,9], optional [9], forced a=7. Greedy: pos1 use9, pos2 S=9 no, pos3 S=9 no, forced unused, a not in res, set pos3=7 ->997. Is optimal? Tokens mandatory7, optional9. Use9 pos1, forced7 pos3 =>997. Alternative forced7 pos1,9 pos2? pos2 S=9 no improve =>799, worse. 997 best. Good.

Now, consider if optional token >a at early and a later S=a, forced unused no change. Example S=[1,7,9], optional [9], forced a=7. Greedy: pos1 use9, pos2 S=7 no d>7? forced7 not >, no -> keep7, pos3 S=9 no, forced unused, a in res pos2, do nothing ->979. Is optimal? Use9 pos1, forced7 pos2 original7 no change =>979. Alternative forced7 pos1,9 pos2? pos2 S=7, 9>7 ->799? Actually pos1=7,pos2=9,pos3=9 =>799, worse. 979 best. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where using optional token at a position with S < optional but optional digit is less than forced a, and forced a not used yet. Greedy would use forced a at that position instead, because a larger. Example S=[1,1], optional5, forced7. Greedy pos1 forced7, pos2 optional5 ->75. Alternative optional5 pos1, forced7 pos2 =>57, worse. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where optional token digit equals forced a but forced unused, and S[i] < a. Greedy uses forced. If there are multiple such positions, forced used at first, optional a used at second. Example S=[1,1], T has a=7 and opt7. Greedy pos1 forced7, pos2 opt7 ->77. Alternative opt7 pos1, forced7 pos2 ->77. Same. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where forced a is high but there is an optional token higher, and using optional higher at first position leaves forced for second; final maybe higher at first, a at second. Alternative forced at first, optional higher at second: first a < higher, so greedy better. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where using optional higher at first position prevents using forced at a later position with S=a no change, causing forced to overwrite a high optional at rightmost; but first digit higher still dominates. Example S=[1,9,1], optional9, forced7. Greedy: pos1 use9, pos2 S=9 no, pos3 S=1 forced7>1 use forced -> [9,9,7] =997. Alternative forced7 pos1, optional9 pos3 => [7,9,9]=799, worse. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where using optional higher at first position and forced at end rightmost yields final [9,9,7], while alternative forced at first and optional higher at second yields [7,9,9]; 997 better. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where using optional higher at first position and forced at end rightmost yields final [9,1,7], while alternative forced at first and optional higher at second yields [7,9,9]? Compare [9,1,7] vs [7,9,9]: first 9>7, so greedy better even though second lower. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where using optional higher at first position consumes the only higher token, and forced at end overwrites a later position that was improved by a lower token; maybe alternative forced at first and higher token later yields same first? No, first higher vs forced lower, so not same. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where S[i] is already higher than all tokens, but forced unused and a not in res, set rightmost. Could using forced at a position with S[i] > a but not rightmost allow an optional token to be used at rightmost to improve it, yielding better? Example S=[9,1], optional8, forced7. Greedy: pos1 S=9 no, pos2 S=1 candidates8 opt,7 forced -> best8 use opt -> [9,8]; forced unused, a=7 not in res, set pos2=7 ->97. Alternative forced7 pos1, optional8 pos2 => [7,8] =78, worse. Alternative optional8 pos1? S1=9 no improve, forced7 pos2 =>97. Good.
S=[9,1], optional9, forced7. Greedy: pos1 S=9 no, pos2 S=1 best9 opt -> [9,9]; forced unused, a=7 not in res, set pos2=7 ->97. Alternative forced7 pos1, optional9 pos2 =>79, worse. Good.
S=[9,1], optional6, forced7. Greedy: pos1 no, pos2 S=1 candidates7 forced,6 opt -> best7 forced -> [9,7]. Output97. Alternative optional6 pos1? no, forced7 pos1 =>79, worse. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where S[i] > a at an early position, and a later position S<a but no optional >a, greedy uses forced at later, good. If there is optional >a at later, greedy uses optional at later, forced unused, end maybe rightmost. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where S[i] > a at early, later S<a with optional >a, and rightmost S>a. Greedy uses optional at later, forced at rightmost. Final early high, later optional high, rightmost a. Alternative forced at later, optional at rightmost? rightmost S>a, optional maybe >rightmost? If optional > rightmost, could improve rightmost; but later forced a vs optional high at later: later position more significant than rightmost, so greedy better. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where optional token >a at later, and rightmost S<a but no optional, forced would be used at rightmost if not used? Actually if rightmost S<a and forced unused, greedy would use forced at rightmost, so forced not unused. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where forced unused and a not in res, but there is an earlier position with S[i] < a and optional token >a used, and rightmost S < a? If rightmost S<a and forced unused, greedy would have used forced at rightmost when processing it, unless there was optional >a at rightmost too. If optional >a at rightmost used, then rightmost final >a. So all good.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm should use optional tokens even if forced_used False and S[i] < a but optional token >a available; yes because larger. If optional token >a not available but optional token =a available, use forced (same digit). If optional token <a available, use forced. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where using forced at a position with S[i] < a but there is optional token >a available, and using optional >a now would make forced unused and later forced at a position with S=a no change, yielding same prefix? No, prefix with optional >a is larger than forced a, so better. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where using optional >a now consumes a token that could have been used to prevent forced damage at end, but forced damage at end is at rightmost; if optional >a used at an earlier position, it's not at rightmost, so forced damage still at rightmost. If optional >a saved for rightmost, forced could be placed at earlier S=a no change? But if earlier S=a, forced no change; optional at rightmost improves rightmost. Compare using optional at earlier vs rightmost:
- Option A: optional at earlier (pos i), forced at rightmost (pos j) or S=a no change. Final pos i = H, pos j = a (if no S=a).
- Option B: forced at pos i (if S[i]<a? then a < H), optional at pos j = H. Final pos i = a, pos j = H.
Since i<j, Option A better at pos i. So using optional earlier is better. If forced could be placed at S=a no change, Option A final pos i=H, pos j maybe original; Option B pos i=a, pos j=H; Option A better. So greedy correct.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where optional >a used at an earlier position, and forced at end rightmost, but there is an even earlier position with S=a no change; forced no change, final optional earlier. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where optional >a used at an earlier position, and forced at end rightmost, but rightmost was improved by another optional token; forced overwrites it. Could we instead not use that rightmost optional token and use it at an earlier position that was kept because no token? If earlier position kept, it means no available token > S[i] at that time. But if rightmost optional token H was available then, and H > S[i], greedy would have used H earlier. Unless a larger token was used there. If larger token used, final >H. If no token used, H would have been used. So no earlier position can be improved by H. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where rightmost optional token H was used at rightmost because at all earlier positions S >= H. Then if forced overwrites rightmost, H discarded. Could H be used at an earlier position where S = H? No improvement, no need. Where S < H? None, because if S < H, greedy would have used H (or larger) there. So no alternative. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where rightmost optional token H was used at rightmost, but an earlier position had S < H and was improved by a larger token G. If H discarded, could H replace G at that earlier position and G move to rightmost? That would make earlier digit H < G, worse. So no.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where rightmost optional token H was used at rightmost, and an earlier position had S < H and was improved by a token L < H? Greedy would have used H instead of L at that earlier position because H available and larger. So no.

Thus end placement optimal.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using `range(9, s, -1)` when s is int from bytes; if s=9, range(9,9,-1) empty. If s=1, range(9,1,-1) gives 9,8,7,6,5,4,3,2. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possibility that T has length M but M maybe 0? Constraints M≥1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a hidden issue with "digits from 1 to 9" and "integer" maybe if N large, but output string. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a known alternative: We could process T from right to left maintaining max? But our solution okay.

Let's maybe try to prove the reduction more formally in case final explanation needed, but final code only. Still, let's ensure no overlooked constraint: The operation sequence is for k=1..M in order. We choose i at each step. We know T. We assumed final assignment can be any subset K containing M. But is it possible that a selected time k assigned to position p, and all non-selected times assigned to M's position q, but if there are multiple selected times, at a selected time l, we choose position r. What about non-selected times before l assigned to q; okay. But what if q is also assigned a selected time M, and we choose q at non-selected times before M. At time M, q final T[M]. Good. No conflict. So any subset K containing M feasible. This is a strong property. Let's test with K={2,4} M=4, positions p2,p4. Schedule: t=1 non-selected choose p4; t=2 selected choose p2; t=3 non-selected choose p4; t=4 selected choose p4. Final p2=T2, p4=T4. Feasible. If K={1,4}: t=1 selected p1; t=2,3 non-selected p4; t=4 selected p4. Feasible. If K={3,4}: t=1,2 non-selected p4; t=3 selected p3; t=4 selected p4. Feasible. If K={2,3,4}: t=1 non-selected p4; t=2 p2; t=3 p3; t=4 p4. Feasible. So yes.

Now, let's think about if we can choose K size = N but M maybe less than N? K size ≤ M because subset of M times. Counts sum M. Greedy uses at most min(N,M) tokens because each position one and counts. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where using a token at a position with S[i] < d but d is from a T index that is before another selected token assigned to a more significant position? Order of selected times doesn't matter for final assignment, as shown. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where the optimal final string uses a T digit at a position but the corresponding selected time is after M? impossible.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where the optimal final string uses no T digit from T[M]? impossible because operation M changes some position to T[M]. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where the optimal final string uses T[M] at a position but also uses another T digit at the same position from an earlier time, and that earlier time is selected? No, if earlier time at same position, it would be overwritten by M, so not final. Our model doesn't allow two tokens per position. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where an optional token is used at a position and later a non-selected operation (not M) overwrites it, but the final digit still from optional token because later overwritten by another operation? If overwritten by non-selected operation, then that non-selected operation's time would be later and would become the last selected time for that position unless overwritten again. Ultimately the last operation at that position is a selected time in K. So final digit from the last operation at that position. Our model selects that last operation. Earlier operations at same position are non-selected. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where a position is touched multiple times, and the final digit is original S because the last operation at that position sets it to original S? But operations only set T digits, not original S. If a position is touched, final digit is T[last], not original. So original final positions are untouched. Our model. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where a position is touched and the last operation's T digit equals original S, so final digit same as original but position is touched. In our model, we could either include that time in K or not; final string same. If we include it, position is in P but digit same. Our algorithm may place forced at such position no change. Optional tokens equal to S are not used because no benefit. But if an optional token equal to S is used and later overwritten by forced? It doesn't affect final. Our model ignores it. Feasible. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where using an optional token equal to S at a position could "waste" an operation and allow a different operation to be selected? Operations are all performed regardless; we can always waste on M's position. No need to use equal tokens. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where the algorithm's final string might not be achievable because it uses optional tokens but not enough non-selected operations to fill all times? There are M - |K| non-selected times. We assign them all to M's position. This is valid regardless of how many. If |K|=M (all times selected), no non-selected times. Feasible: at each time choose its assigned position. If |K|<M, assign non-selected to M's position. But wait if a non-selected time t is assigned to M's position q, and q is also selected at time M. If t < M, okay. If t > M? none. So yes. If |K|=M, then M selected and all times selected; no non-selected. Feasible. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where |K|=M and N < M? Then |K|≤N required. If M>N, cannot select all times because not enough positions. Our algorithm uses at most N tokens; counts may have more but unused. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where the optimal final string uses fewer than N positions but more than M? impossible.

Now, let's think about if there is a hidden issue with "Choose an integer i such that 1 ≤ i ≤ N" at each step; if N large, okay.

Now, let's consider if there is a possibility to improve final string by choosing a position at time k that will be overwritten at time M, and then at time M choosing a different position, effectively using T[k] as a "waste" but maybe T[k] is high and could have been used final if not overwritten. Our model chooses selected times; if T[k] high and we want it final, we assign it to a position not overwritten. If we don't, it's non-selected and can be wasted on M's position. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where a high T[k] appears before M, and we want to use it final, but if we assign all non-selected times to M's position, at time k we choose the high token's position. That's fine. But what if there is another selected time l > k assigned to a position, and a non-selected time between k and l assigned to M's position; no conflict. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where a high T[k] appears before M, but using it final at position p means p cannot be used as waste for non-selected times after k; we use M's position for waste. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where M's position q is also used for waste before M, and at some selected time k, we choose p. At time k, q not chosen. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where selected time k is before M, and we assign all non-selected times to q. At time k, if k is also a non-selected time for q? No, k selected for p. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where selected time k and selected time l are both before M, and k<l. At time k choose p, at time l choose r. Non-selected times between choose q. Feasible. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where selected time k is assigned to position p, and p is also chosen at a non-selected time before k? We assign non-selected to q, so no. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where selected time k is assigned to position p, and p is also chosen at a non-selected time after k? We assign non-selected to q, so no. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a scenario where selected time M is assigned to q, and q is also chosen at a selected time before M? No, selected times distinct positions. Good.

Thus feasibility solid.

Now, let's think about if the algorithm's greedy proof should account for the fact that using a token at position i might make it impossible to use another token at position i because one per position; but we only use one per position. Good.

No, using lower at current makes current worse; mandatory can be placed later. If no later and mandatory must be placed, choose rightmost, but not at an early position if it decreases. Greedy doesn't use lower at current. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where forced unused and a in res, but the position with a is before some position that was improved by optional token. We do nothing, final optional remains. Could there be a better final by placing forced at that optional-improved position and using the optional token at the a position? The a position S=a, optional token >a would improve it, but then forced at optional-improved position would decrease it. Compare:
- Option A (no change): pos i = a, pos j = H (j>i)
- Option B: pos i = H, pos j = a
Since i<j, Option B has H>a at i, so Option B is better! Wait, this is important. Let's examine.

We assumed if a in res, placing forced at that position no change is optimal. But what if we could move an optional token from a later position to the a position, and place forced at the later position, yielding a higher digit at the earlier a position? That might be better! Did our algorithm consider this? Let's construct a concrete example.

We need forced unused, a in res at position i (original S=a, not replaced). There is a later position j with an optional token H > a used. Option A (greedy end no change): final at i = a, at j = H. Option B: use optional H at i (improve a to H), place forced at j (set to a). Final at i = H, at j = a. Since i<j, Option B is lexicographically larger! Is Option B feasible? We would be using optional token H at position i (more significant) instead of j, and forced at j. This seems better. Did our greedy algorithm miss this? Let's test with actual numbers.

Example: N=2, S=[5,1], T? Need forced a=5, optional H=9. S=[5,1]. Greedy earlier: pos1 S=5, opt9>5 use9 -> [9,1]; pos2 S=1, forced5>1 use forced -> [9,5]. forced used, not unused. So not this case.

Need forced unused and a in res at i, with later optional H used. For forced unused, at every position with S<a, forced would be used unless optional >a used. If i has S=a, not S<a. Later j with optional H used. But if later j has S < a, then forced would be used at j unless optional >a used. If optional >a used at j, forced remains unused. Then a in res at i. This is exactly S=[5,1], a=5, optional H=9. Greedy: pos1 S=5, opt9>5 use9 (not keep a). Wait i is pos1 S=5=a, but optional H=9 > S[i], so greedy uses H at i, not keep a. So a in res at i is lost. Thus the scenario "a in res at i and later optional H used" with i earlier than j cannot happen if optional H > a and S[i]=a, because greedy would use H at i (since H > S[i]) before reaching j. Unless H was not available at i? Counts available from start, so yes available. If H is used at j, it was available at i. Greedy would have used H at i instead of keeping a. Therefore a in res at i implies no optional token > S[i]=a was available at i, or if available, it was used at i. Since H used later, it was available, so greedy would use it at i. Thus the scenario is impossible. Good! This is a key point.

What if i is after j? If a in res at i (later) and optional H used at j (earlier), Option B would move H from earlier to later and forced to earlier? Compare:
- Option A: pos j = H, pos i = a (i>j)
- Option B: pos j = a, pos i = H
Since j<i, Option A has H>a at j, so Option A better. Greedy no change is optimal. Good.

So the only potentially better swap is moving optional token from later to earlier a position, but greedy would have used it earlier if available. Thus no issue.

Let's test a more complex scenario: S=[5,1,1], a=5, optional H=9, maybe another token. Greedy:
pos1 S=5, opt9>5 use9 -> [9,1,1]
pos2 S=1, forced5>1 use forced -> [9,5,1]
pos3 S=1, no tokens -> [9,5,1]. forced used, not unused.
If optional H=9 and no forced at pos2? forced used. Good.

What if S=[5,9,1], a=5, optional H=9? Greedy:
pos1 S=5, opt9>5 use9 -> [9,9,1]
pos2 S=9 no
pos3 S=1, forced5>1 use forced -> [9,9,5]. forced used.
If S=[5,9,9], a=5, opt9. Greedy:
pos1 use9 -> [9,9,9]
pos2 no, pos3 no, forced unused, a not in res, set pos3=5 ->995.
Could we instead keep pos1=5, use9 at pos2? S2=9 no improve, forced pos3=5 ->595; worse. Use9 pos1, forced pos3=5 ->995. Good.
If S=[5,8,9], a=5, opt9. Greedy:
pos1 use9 -> [9,8,9]
pos2 S=8, no opt? opt used, forced5 not >8 -> keep8
pos3 S=9 no, forced unused, a not in res, set pos3=5 ->985.
Alternative keep pos1=5, use9 at pos2 (8<9) -> [5,9,9]? forced5 at pos3? final [5,9,5]=595, worse. Greedy better.
So no counterexample.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where a in res at position i, and later optional H used at j, but H was not available at i because H's count was 0 at i and later became available? Counts don't increase. So impossible. Good.

So if forced unused and a in res, indeed no optional tokens are used? Let's prove: If any optional token d is used at some position j. If the a position is after j, then optional used earlier, a later; no swap issue. - Greedy no change: pos1=9, pos2=5 =>95.
- Alternative: forced5 at pos1 (S1=1<5), H9 at pos2 (S2=5<9) =>59. Greedy better because pos1 9>5. So no issue. The swap would move H to later, worse.
What if a in res at pos1 and optional H used later impossible because H would be used at pos1. So the only possible optional tokens used when a in res are >a and before the a position. Greedy no change is optimal because moving H later would decrease earlier position. Good.

Thus end placement no change is optimal.

Now, let's think about if there is a scenario where a in res at a position that was originally S=a but we used optional at it with digit >a and then later
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    N = int(data[0])
    M = int(data[1])
    S = data[2]
    T = data[3]

    cnt = [0] * 10
    for b in T:
        cnt[b - 48] += 1

    a = T[-1] - 48
    opt = cnt[:]
    opt[a] -= 1

    forced_used = False
    res = bytearray(N)

    for idx, b in enumerate(S):
        s = b - 48
        best = -1

        for d in range(9, s, -1):
            if opt[d] > 0 or (not forced_used and d == a):
                best = d
                break

        if best != -1:
            res[idx] = 48 + best
            if best == a and not forced_used:
                forced_used = True
            else:
                opt[best] -= 1
        else:
            res[idx] = b

    if not forced_used:
        if (48 + a) not in res:
            res[-1] = 48 + a

    sys.stdout.buffer.write(res + b'\n')

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
